They were keeping tabs on my paintings and my client papers because they wanted to steal a chance that was meant for me. People kept telling me it made absolutely no sense at all, but I didn't care what they said, I still knew they were doing it. \n\nThey really wanted my paintings, I think. I mostly do watercolors, you know. Landscapes, little birds, things like that. I get my paints from this little craft shop over on 4th street. The owner there, he has this lazy eye, so you never really know if he's looking right at you or at the cash register. Good man, though. He sells these expensive brushes, but honestly, they shed worse than a golden retriever in July. I was painting this lovely little sunset for Mrs. Higgins just last week, and I swear I spent half the time picking little hairs out of the wet paint with my fingernail. She didn't mind, though. She hung it right up on her refrigerator with a magnet shaped like a strawberry. I bought her that magnet for her birthday two years ago. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the watching. Yes, they definitely were watching my things, no matter what anybody else tried to tell me.\n\nDoctor: Are those beliefs about being watched and having your work stolen something that has always felt completely true to you, even though others don’t see it the same way?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Like I've been telling you, it was a hundred percent true to me. People kept saying I wasn't making a lick of sense, but I knew in my bones that the other agency and those library folks were watching my paintings and my client papers to steal that chance meant for me. \n\nAnd my client papers, well, I keep them all very organized in this big blue binder. It's one of those heavy-duty ones with the thick metal rings. I bought it at that office supply place off the highway, the one right next to the old diner that closed down last year. It is a real shame about that diner, actually. They made the absolute best cherry pie. The crust was always so flaky, not like that soggy frozen stuff you get at the grocery store nowadays. My mom used to make pies from scratch, too. Mostly apple, though. She had this special wooden rolling pin with bright red handles. I think my sister took it when mom passed away. My sister always was a bit of a grabber, you know? She took the good silverware, too, just packed it right up in the trunk of her sedan before we even finished sorting the house. \n\nBut anyway, yes, I knew for a fact they were watching my things, and I didn't care who told me otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that most people would find strange or impossible, like having thoughts inserted into your mind, being controlled by outside forces, or that something has special meaning just for you that others don’t understand?\n\nPatient: Well, like having thoughts put in my head? I don't know about all that, but it's like I've been saying, about the special meaning and people not understanding... it's just that I got it in my head that the other home-care agency and the library people were watching my paintings and my client papers. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. And even when folks told me it made absolutely no sense, I still knew they were doing it. \n\nSpeaking of chances, I almost had a chance to go to Florida once. This was years ago, back when I was still married to Richard. He had this buddy who owned a timeshare down near Orlando, right by the beach. Richard loved the beach. He used to get these terrible sunburns, though, peeling all over his shoulders like a snake shedding its skin. He never would put on sunscreen, said it smelled like coconuts and chemicals. I always use the unscented kind myself. I have very sensitive skin, you see. If I use anything with perfume in it, I break out in these little red bumps all along my collarbone. It's a real nuisance, especially in the summer when you want to wear a nice scoop-neck blouse. I have this one blouse, a lovely peach color, but I can hardly ever wear it because of the sunscreen issue. \n\nBut what was I... oh, the library people. Right. Yes, they were watching my things, and I just knew it, no matter what anybody said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like thoughts were being placed into your mind that aren’t yours, or that something outside of yourself was trying to control your actions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like someone is putting thoughts in there with a machine or anything. It's just... well, like I've been trying to explain, it's about the watching. I just got it in my head—and it was the truth, I don't care who said otherwise—that the other home-care agency and those library people were watching my paintings. And my client papers, too. They wanted to steal this chance that was meant for me. People kept saying, \"Oh, that makes no sense,\" but I knew they were doing it. \n\nI actually used to go to that library all the time. The one downtown, with the big stone lions out front. They're very majestic, those lions. I always wanted a dog that looked a bit like a lion, maybe a Chow Chow. They have those purple tongues, you know. I read once that giraffes have purple tongues, too, so they don't get sunburned while they eat leaves off the tall trees. I saw a giraffe at the zoo when I was a little girl, maybe eight or nine. My uncle took me. He bought me a big pink cotton candy, and it got all stuck in my hair. My mother was so mad when I got home, she had to wash my hair three times in the kitchen sink with dish soap just to get the sticky out. It smelled like lemon Joy for a week. \n\nBut anyway, no, nobody is controlling my actions. It's just the library people and that other agency, watching my things to steal my chance. I just know it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like everyday things, like something on TV, a song on the radio, or something someone says, is meant specifically for you or has a special hidden meaning just for you?\n\nPatient: Well, a hidden meaning? Not exactly like a secret code on the television, if that's what you mean. We used to have one of those big box televisions, the kind with the dials you had to turn by hand. My dad would make me get up and change the channel for him. \"Be my remote control,\" he'd say. He loved watching those old westerns. Gunsmoke, Bonanza... I always liked the horses. I used to collect little glass horses. I had a whole shelf of them in my bedroom, right above my radiator. One time, the radiator leaked and ruined the paint on the wall. It bubbled all up and peeled off in these long strips that looked like dried out seaweed. I tried to glue it back down with Elmer's glue, but it just made a terrible sticky mess. \n\nBut about the special meaning... it's just what I keep telling you. It's not a song on the radio. It wasn't a hidden message, it was just the truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that turned out not to be true, something that most people would find very strange or impossible, even if they tried to explain it to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I've been saying, it's just that whole thing with the library people and the other agency. And my client papers, too. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. \n\nI used to paint a lot of landscapes, mostly water scenes. I love the color blue. Cerulean, navy, aqua... there are so many shades. I had this one tube of paint, ultramarine, that I bought at a little art supply store that used to be on Main Street. It closed down, though, maybe five years ago? The owner, Mr. Henderson, he retired and moved to Arizona. Said his arthritis couldn't take the cold winters here anymore. I don't blame him. He sells these expensive brushes, but honestly, they shed worse than a golden retriever in July. I was painting this lovely little sunset for Mrs. Higgins just last week, and I swear I spent half the time picking little hairs out of the wet paint with my fingernail. She didn't mind, though. She hung it right up on her refrigerator with a magnet shaped like a strawberry. I bought her that magnet for her birthday two years ago. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the watching. Yes, they definitely were watching my things, no matter what anybody else tried to tell me.\n\nDoctor: Are those beliefs about being watched and having your work stolen something that has always felt completely true to you, even though others don’t see it the same way?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Like I've been telling you, it was a hundred percent true to me. People kept saying I wasn't making a lick of sense, but I knew in my bones that the other agency and those library folks were watching my paintings and my client papers to steal that chance meant for me. \n\nAnd my client papers, well, I keep them all very organized in this big blue binder. It's one of those heavy-duty ones with the thick metal rings. I bought it at that office supply place off the highway, the one right next to the old diner that closed down last year. It is a real shame about that diner, actually. They made the absolute best cherry pie. The crust was always so flaky, not like that soggy frozen stuff you get at the grocery store nowadays. My mom used to make pies from scratch, too. Mostly apple, though. She had this special wooden rolling pin with bright red handles. I think my sister took it when mom passed away. My sister always was a bit of a grabber, you know? She took the good silverware, too, just packed it right up in the trunk of her sedan before we even finished sorting the house. \n\nBut anyway, yes, I knew for a fact they were watching my things, and I didn't care who told me otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that most people would find strange or impossible, like having thoughts inserted into your mind, being controlled by outside forces, or that something has special meaning just for you that others don’t understand?\n\nPatient: Well, like having thoughts put in my head? I don't know about all that, but it's like I've been saying, about the special meaning and people not understanding... it's just that I got it in my head that the other home-care agency and the library people were watching my paintings and my client papers. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. And even when folks told me it made absolutely no sense, I still knew they were doing it. \n\nSpeaking of chances, I almost had a chance to go to Florida once. This was years ago, back when I was still married to Richard. He had this buddy who owned a timeshare down near Orlando, right by the beach. Richard loved the beach. He used to get these terrible sunburns, though, peeling all over his shoulders like a snake shedding its skin. He never would put on sunscreen, said it smelled like coconuts and chemicals. I always use the unscented kind myself. I have very sensitive skin, you see. If I use anything with perfume in it, I break out in these little red bumps all along my collarbone. It's a real nuisance, especially in the summer when you want to wear a nice scoop-neck blouse. I have this one blouse, a lovely peach color, but I can hardly ever wear it because of the sunscreen issue. \n\nBut what was I... oh, the library people. Right. Yes, they were watching my things, and I just knew it, no matter what anybody said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like thoughts were being placed into your mind that aren’t yours, or that something outside of yourself was trying to control your actions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like someone is putting thoughts in there with a machine or anything. It's just... well, like I've been trying to explain, it's about the watching. I just got it in my head—and it was the truth, I don't care who said otherwise—that the other home-care agency and those library people were watching my paintings. And my client papers, too. They wanted to steal this chance that was meant for me. People kept saying, \"Oh, that makes no sense,\" but I knew they were doing it. \n\nI actually used to go to that library all the time. The one downtown, with the big stone lions out front. They're very majestic, those lions. I always wanted a dog that looked a bit like a lion, maybe a Chow Chow. They have those purple tongues, you know. I read once that giraffes have purple tongues, too, so they don't get sunburned while they eat leaves off the tall trees. I saw a giraffe at the zoo when I was a little girl, maybe eight or nine. My uncle took me. He bought me a big pink cotton candy, and it got all stuck in my hair. My mother was so mad when I got home, she had to wash my hair three times in the kitchen sink with dish soap just to get the sticky out. It smelled like lemon Joy for a week. \n\nBut anyway, no, nobody is controlling my actions. It's just the library people and that other agency, watching my things to steal my chance. I just know it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like everyday things, like something on TV, a song on the radio, or something someone says, is meant specifically for you or has a special hidden meaning just for you?\n\nPatient: Well, a hidden meaning? Not exactly like a secret code on the television, if that's what you mean. We used to have one of those big box televisions, the kind with the dials you had to turn by hand. My dad would make me get up and change the channel for him. \"Be my remote control,\" he'd say. He loved watching those old westerns. Gunsmoke, Bonanza... I always liked the horses. I used to collect little glass horses. I had a whole shelf of them in my bedroom, right above my radiator. One time, the radiator leaked and ruined the paint on the wall. It bubbled all up and peeled off in these long strips that looked like dried out seaweed. I tried to glue it back down with Elmer's glue, but it just made a terrible sticky mess. \n\nBut about the special meaning... it's just what I keep telling you. It's not a song on the radio. It's that I got it in my head that the other home-care agency and those people from the library were watching my paintings and my client papers. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. And even when folks told me it made absolutely no sense, I still knew they were doing it. It wasn't a hidden message, it was just the truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that turned out not to be true, something that most people would find very strange or impossible, even if they tried to explain it to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I've been saying, it's just that whole thing with the library people and the other agency. I know folks thought it was impossible, they kept telling me it made absolutely no sense at all. But I just got it in my head—and it was true—that they were watching my paintings. And my client papers, too. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. \n\nI used to paint a lot of landscapes, mostly water scenes. I love the color blue. Cerulean, navy, aqua... there are so many shades. I had this one tube of paint, ultramarine, that I bought at a little art supply store that used to be on Main Street. It closed down, though, maybe five years ago? The owner, Mr. Henderson, he retired and moved to Arizona. Said his arthritis couldn't take the cold winters here anymore. I don't blame him. My knees ache terribly when it rains. I have to take a hot bath with Epsom salts just to get moving some mornings. The lavender scented salts are the best, very relaxing. I tried the eucalyptus once, but it just smelled like cough drops. Reminded me of being sick as a kid. My mother would rub Vicks VapoRub all over my chest and make me wear an old wool sock around my neck. It was terribly itchy. \n\nBut yes, even with everyone telling me I was wrong, I knew they were watching my things. I just knew it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that even you realized was very strange or didn’t make sense in the real world, something that most people would find completely impossible?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I don't think it's impossible. People said it was strange, sure. They told me it made absolutely no sense at all. But I just got it in my head that the other home-care agency and those people from the library were watching my paintings. And my client papers, too. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. And even when folks told me it made no sense, I still knew they were doing it. \n\nI actually used to keep my paintings in a big cardboard box under my bed. It was a refrigerator box, from when my neighbor got a new Frigidaire. It was avocado green. You don't see appliances in those colors anymore. Everything now is stainless steel. It shows every single fingerprint. I was cleaning a stainless steel fridge at my client's house last week—Mrs. Gable, she's ninety-two and sharp as a tack, but she can't reach the top shelves anymore. I had to use a step stool, and one of the legs wobbled. I almost fell right into the kitchen island. It's granite, you know. Very hard. If I hit my head on that, I'd have a knot the size of a grapefruit. I love grapefruit, especially the ruby red ones with a little sprinkle of sugar on top. My grandmother used to serve them in these special little glass bowls with a serrated spoon. \n\nBut anyway, no, it wasn't impossible. It was just the truth about the library people and the agency. I knew what they were up to, no matter what anyone else said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts were not entirely your own, or that something outside of you was trying to control what you think or do?\n\nPatient: No, I've never felt anything like that. My thoughts are my own. I mean, nobody's controlling what I think. I do think a lot, though. I'm always thinking about what I need to get done at work. I have a list I keep in my purse. It's a leather purse, real heavy even when it's empty. My daughter bought it for me for Mother's Day a few years ago. She lives out in Ohio now. It's so flat out there. We drove through once when she was little, and you could see for miles and miles just cornfields. I don't know how people don't get lost when everything looks exactly the same. We stopped at this little diner off the highway, and I had the best slice of cherry pie. The crust was so flaky, not soggy at all. I hate a soggy pie crust. It ruins the whole thing. I tried to make one from scratch once, but I used too much water and the dough just turned into paste. \n\nBut no, nobody is putting thoughts in my head or controlling me. It's just my own thinking.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail why you were so sure that the other home-care agency and the library people were watching your paintings and client papers? What made you certain they were trying to steal a chance that was meant for you?\n\nPatient: Well, it was just... I don't know how to explain it other than I just got it in my head. I knew it. The other home-care agency and the library people, they were watching my paintings, and they were watching my client papers, too. I just knew they were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. People kept telling me it made no sense, but I knew they were doing it. \n\nYou know, I kept those client papers in a special folder. It was a bright yellow one, plastic, so if I spilled my coffee on it, it wouldn't ruin everything. I drink a lot of coffee. Usually just black, but sometimes I like a little splash of half-and-half. The store near my house was out of half-and-half last Tuesday, so I had to buy heavy cream. It made the coffee so rich, almost like a dessert. I try not to eat too many desserts, though. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he said my cholesterol is creeping up. He wants me to eat more oatmeal. I hate oatmeal. It's like eating wet cement. Unless you put a ton of brown sugar and raisins in it, but then that defeats the whole purpose, doesn't it? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just knew they were watching my things. I could feel it, even if nobody else believed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that most people would consider impossible or not based in reality, like being given special messages from space or having a hidden identity that no one else knows about?\n\nPatient: Messages from space? No, nothing like that. I don't have any hidden identity either, I'm just me. I mean, I'm just a personal care aide. I don't know anything about space. I did see a shooting star once, though. I was sitting on my back porch—it's screened in, because the mosquitoes get so bad in the summer. They just eat me alive. My blood must be sweet or something. I bought one of those bug zappers, the ones with the blue light? It makes this loud crack every time a bug flies into it. It scared my neighbor's cat so bad the first time he heard it, he jumped right over the fence. He's an orange tabby, very friendly, but he sheds everywhere. I tried to pet him last week and my black pants were just covered in orange fur. I had to use half a roll of lint tape before I could go to work. You can't show up to a client's house looking like a mess. \n\nBut no, no messages from space or secret identities or anything impossible like that. Just the library people watching my things, like I said.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Just the library people watching my things, like I said.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had any beliefs that most people would find completely impossible or out of touch with reality, even if they tried to explain things to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 55 / personal_care_aide / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I got it in my head that the other home-care agency and some people from the library were watching my paintings and client papers to steal a chance that was meant for me, and even when folks told me it made no sense I still knew they were doing it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't think I've had anything completely impossible happen. But like I told you before, I did get it in my head that the other home-care agency and some people from the library were watching my paintings and my client papers. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. Folks kept telling me it made no sense, but I still knew they were doing it. 

I don't know why people from the library would even care about paintings, honestly. The library downtown is so quiet, you know? I used to go there when my daughter was little to check out those picture books. The ones with the shiny covers. They always smelled like old paper and floor wax. I love the smell of floor wax. It reminds me of the elementary school I went to. Mr. Henderson was the janitor, and he always had a big ring of keys jingling on his belt. He let me help him clap the chalkboard erasers outside once. You don't see chalkboards much anymore, do you? Everything is whiteboards and those special markers. They dry out so fast if you don't put the cap back on tight. I bought a pack for my kitchen calendar to write down my shifts, but the red one dried up in two days. I was so mad. They cost like six dollars at the pharmacy. 

But anyway, yeah, I just knew they were watching my things, even if people said it wasn't real.

They keep saying my name and telling me my work is being judged, even when the house is completely quiet and nobody is there. It's just so distracting, you know? Especially when I'm trying to get ready for work. I help out some older folks, make sure they're fed and bathed, that sort of thing. I've been doing it for years. People kept telling me it made absolutely no sense at all, but I didn't care what they said, I still knew they were doing it. \n\nThey really wanted my paintings, I think. Like I've been telling you, it was a hundred percent true to me. People kept saying I wasn't making a lick of sense, but I knew in my bones that the other agency and those library folks were watching my paintings and my client papers to steal that chance meant for me. \n\nAnd my client papers, well, I keep them all very organized in this big blue binder. It's one of those heavy-duty ones with the thick metal rings. I bought it at that office supply place off the highway, the one right next to the old diner that closed down last year. I don't know about all that, but it's like I've been saying, about the special meaning and people not understanding... it's just that I got it in my head that the other home-care agency and the library people were watching my paintings and my client papers. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. And even when folks told me it made absolutely no sense, I still knew they were doing it. \n\nSpeaking of chances, I almost had a chance to go to Florida once. This was years ago, back when I was still married to Richard. He had this buddy who owned a timeshare down near Orlando, right by the beach. Richard loved the beach. He used to get these terrible sunburns, though, peeling all over his shoulders like a snake shedding its skin. He never would put on sunscreen, said it smelled like coconuts and chemicals. I always use the unscented kind myself. I have very sensitive skin, you see. If I use anything with perfume in it, I break out in these little red bumps all along my collarbone. It's a real nuisance, especially in the summer when you want to wear a nice scoop-neck blouse. I have this one blouse, a lovely peach color, but I can hardly ever wear it because of the sunscreen issue. \n\nBut what was I... oh, the library people. Right. Yes, they were watching my things, and I just knew it, no matter what anybody said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like thoughts were being placed into your mind that aren’t yours, or that something outside of yourself was trying to control your actions or thoughts?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like someone is putting thoughts in there with a machine or anything. It's just... well, like I've been trying to explain, it's about the watching. I just got it in my head—and it was the truth, I don't care who said otherwise—that the other home-care agency and those library people were watching my paintings. And my client papers, too. They wanted to steal this chance that was meant for me. People kept saying, \"Oh, that makes no sense,\" but I knew they were doing it. \n\nI actually used to go to that library all the time. The one downtown, with the big stone lions out front. They're very majestic, those lions. I always wanted a dog that looked a bit like a lion, maybe a Chow Chow. They have those purple tongues, you know. I read once that giraffes have purple tongues, too, so they don't get sunburned while they eat leaves off the tall trees. I saw a giraffe at the zoo when I was a little girl, maybe eight or nine. My uncle took me. He bought me a big pink cotton candy, and it got all stuck in my hair. My mother was so mad when I got home, she had to wash my hair three times in the kitchen sink with dish soap just to get the sticky out. It smelled like lemon Joy for a week. \n\nBut anyway, no, nobody is controlling my actions. It's just the library people and that other agency, watching my things to steal my chance. I just know it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like everyday things, like something on TV, a song on the radio, or something someone says, is meant specifically for you or has a special hidden meaning just for you?\n\nPatient: Well, a hidden meaning? Not exactly like a secret code on the television, if that's what you mean. We used to have one of those big box televisions, the kind with the dials you had to turn by hand. My dad would make me get up and change the channel for him. \"Be my remote control,\" he'd say. He loved watching those old westerns. Gunsmoke, Bonanza... I always liked the horses. I used to collect little glass horses. I had a whole shelf of them in my bedroom, right above my radiator. One time, the radiator leaked and ruined the paint on the wall. It bubbled all up and peeled off in these long strips that looked like dried out seaweed. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. And even when folks told me it made absolutely no sense, I still knew they were doing it. It wasn't a hidden message, it was just the truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that turned out not to be true, something that most people would find very strange or impossible, even if they tried to explain it to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I've been saying, it's just that whole thing with the library people and the other agency. I know folks thought it was impossible, they kept telling me it made absolutely no sense at all. But I just got it in my head—and it was true—that they were watching my paintings. And my client papers, too. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. \n\nI used to paint a lot of landscapes, mostly water scenes. I love the color blue. Cerulean, navy, aqua... there are so many shades. I had this one tube of paint, ultramarine, that I bought at a little art supply store that used to be on Main Street. It closed down, though, maybe five years ago? The owner, Mr. Henderson, he retired and moved to Arizona. Said his arthritis couldn't take the cold winters here anymore. I don't blame him. My knees ache terribly when it rains. I have to take a hot bath with Epsom salts just to get moving some mornings. The lavender scented salts are the best, very relaxing. I tried the eucalyptus once, but it just smelled like cough drops. Reminded me of being sick as a kid. My mother would rub Vicks VapoRub all over my chest and make me wear an old wool sock around my neck. It was terribly itchy. \n\nBut yes, even with everyone telling me I was wrong, I knew they were watching my things. I just knew it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that even you realized was very strange or didn’t make sense in the real world, something that most people would find completely impossible?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I don't think it's impossible. People said it was strange, sure. They told me it made absolutely no sense at all. But I just got it in my head that the other home-care agency and those people from the library were watching my paintings. And my client papers, too. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. And even when folks told me it made no sense, I still knew they were doing it. \n\nI actually used to keep my paintings in a big cardboard box under my bed. It was a refrigerator box, from when my neighbor got a new Frigidaire. It was avocado green. You don't see appliances in those colors anymore. Everything now is stainless steel. It shows every single fingerprint. I was cleaning a stainless steel fridge at my client's house last week—Mrs. Gable, she's ninety-two and sharp as a tack, but she can't reach the top shelves anymore. I had to use a step stool, and one of the legs wobbled. I almost fell right into the kitchen island. It's granite, you know. Very hard. If I hit my head on that, I'd have a knot the size of a grapefruit. I love grapefruit, especially the ruby red ones with a little sprinkle of sugar on top. My grandmother used to serve them in these special little glass bowls with a serrated spoon. \n\nBut anyway, no, it wasn't impossible. It was just the truth about the library people and the agency. I knew what they were up to, no matter what anyone else said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts were not entirely your own, or that something outside of you was trying to control what you think or do?\n\nPatient: No, I've never felt anything like that. My thoughts are my own. I mean, nobody's controlling what I think. I do think a lot, though. I'm always thinking about what I need to get done at work. I have a list I keep in my purse. It's a leather purse, real heavy even when it's empty. My daughter bought it for me for Mother's Day a few years ago. She lives out in Ohio now. It's so flat out there. We drove through once when she was little, and you could see for miles and miles just cornfields. I don't know how people don't get lost when everything looks exactly the same. We stopped at this little diner off the highway, and I had the best slice of cherry pie. The crust was so flaky, not soggy at all. I hate a soggy pie crust. It ruins the whole thing. I tried to make one from scratch once, but I used too much water and the dough just turned into paste. \n\nBut no, nobody is putting thoughts in my head or controlling me. It's just my own thinking.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail why you were so sure that the other home-care agency and the library people were watching your paintings and client papers? What made you certain they were trying to steal a chance that was meant for you?\n\nPatient: Well, it was just... I don't know how to explain it other than I just got it in my head. I knew it. The other home-care agency and the library people, they were watching my paintings, and they were watching my client papers, too. I just knew they were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. People kept telling me it made no sense, but I knew they were doing it. \n\nYou know, I kept those client papers in a special folder. It was a bright yellow one, plastic, so if I spilled my coffee on it, it wouldn't ruin everything. I drink a lot of coffee. Usually just black, but sometimes I like a little splash of half-and-half. The store near my house was out of half-and-half last Tuesday, so I had to buy heavy cream. It made the coffee so rich, almost like a dessert. I try not to eat too many desserts, though. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he said my cholesterol is creeping up. He wants me to eat more oatmeal. I hate oatmeal. It's like eating wet cement. Unless you put a ton of brown sugar and raisins in it, but then that defeats the whole purpose, doesn't it? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just knew they were watching my things. I could feel it, even if nobody else believed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that most people would consider impossible or not based in reality, like being given special messages from space or having a hidden identity that no one else knows about?\n\nPatient: Messages from space? No, nothing like that. I don't have any hidden identity either, I'm just me. I mean, I'm just a personal care aide. I don't know anything about space. I did see a shooting star once, though. I was sitting on my back porch—it's screened in, because the mosquitoes get so bad in the summer. They just eat me alive. My blood must be sweet or something. I bought one of those bug zappers, the ones with the blue light? It makes this loud crack every time a bug flies into it. It scared my neighbor's cat so bad the first time he heard it, he jumped right over the fence. He's an orange tabby, very friendly, but he sheds everywhere. I tried to pet him last week and my black pants were just covered in orange fur. I had to use half a roll of lint tape before I could go to work. You can't show up to a client's house looking like a mess. \n\nBut no, no messages from space or secret identities or anything impossible like that. I saw a giraffe at the zoo when I was a little girl, maybe eight or nine. My uncle took me. He bought me a big pink cotton candy, and it got all stuck in my hair. My mother was so mad when I got home, she had to wash my hair three times in the kitchen sink with dish soap just to get the sticky out. It smelled like lemon Joy for a week. \n\nBut anyway, no, nobody is controlling my actions. It's just the library people and that other agency, watching my things to steal my chance. I just know it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like everyday things, like something on TV, a song on the radio, or something someone says, is meant specifically for you or has a special hidden meaning just for you?\n\nPatient: Well, a hidden meaning? Not exactly like a secret code on the television, if that's what you mean. We used to have one of those big box televisions, the kind with the dials you had to turn by hand. My dad would make me get up and change the channel for him. \"Be my remote control,\" he'd say. He loved watching those old westerns. Gunsmoke, Bonanza... I always liked the horses. I used to collect little glass horses. I had a whole shelf of them in my bedroom, right above my radiator. One time, the radiator leaked and ruined the paint on the wall. It bubbled all up and peeled off in these long strips that looked like dried out seaweed. I tried to glue it back down with Elmer's glue, but it just made a terrible sticky mess. \n\nBut about the special meaning... it's just what I keep telling you. It's not a song on the radio. It's that I got it in my head that the other home-care agency and those people from the library were watching my paintings and my client papers. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. And even when folks told me it made absolutely no sense, I still knew they were doing it. It wasn't a hidden message, it was just the truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something very strongly that turned out not to be true, something that most people would find very strange or impossible, even if they tried to explain it to you?\n\nPatient: Well, like I've been saying, it's just that whole thing with the library people and the other agency. I know folks thought it was impossible, they kept telling me it made absolutely no sense at all. But I just got it in my head—and it was true—that they were watching my paintings. And my client papers, too. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. \n\nI used to paint a lot of landscapes, mostly water scenes. I love the color blue. Cerulean, navy, aqua... there are so many shades. I had this one tube of paint, ultramarine, that I bought at a little art supply store that used to be on Main Street. It closed down, though, maybe five years ago? The owner, Mr. Henderson, he retired and moved to Arizona. Said his arthritis couldn't take the cold winters here anymore. I don't blame him. My knees ache terribly when it rains. I have to take a hot bath with Epsom salts just to get moving some mornings. The lavender scented salts are the best, very relaxing. I tried the eucalyptus once, but it just smelled like cough drops. Reminded me of being sick as a kid. My mother would rub Vicks VapoRub all over my chest and make me wear an old wool sock around my neck. It was terribly itchy. \n\nBut yes, even with everyone telling me I was wrong, I knew they were watching my things. I just knew it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that even you realized was very strange or didn’t make sense in the real world, something that most people would find completely impossible?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I don't think it's impossible. People said it was strange, sure. They told me it made absolutely no sense at all. But I just got it in my head that the other home-care agency and those people from the library were watching my paintings. And my client papers, too. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. And even when folks told me it made no sense, I still knew they were doing it. \n\nI actually used to keep my paintings in a big cardboard box under my bed. It was a refrigerator box, from when my neighbor got a new Frigidaire. It was avocado green. You don't see appliances in those colors anymore. Everything now is stainless steel. It shows every single fingerprint. I was cleaning a stainless steel fridge at my client's house last week—Mrs. Gable, she's ninety-two and sharp as a tack, but she can't reach the top shelves anymore. I had to use a step stool, and one of the legs wobbled. I almost fell right into the kitchen island. It's granite, you know. Very hard. If I hit my head on that, I'd have a knot the size of a grapefruit. I love grapefruit, especially the ruby red ones with a little sprinkle of sugar on top. My grandmother used to serve them in these special little glass bowls with a serrated spoon. \n\nBut anyway, no, it wasn't impossible. It was just the truth about the library people and the agency. I knew what they were up to, no matter what anyone else said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts were not entirely your own, or that something outside of you was trying to control what you think or do?\n\nPatient: No, I've never felt anything like that. My thoughts are my own. I mean, nobody's controlling what I think. I do think a lot, though. I'm always thinking about what I need to get done at work. I have a list I keep in my purse. It's a leather purse, real heavy even when it's empty. My daughter bought it for me for Mother's Day a few years ago. She lives out in Ohio now. It's so flat out there. We drove through once when she was little, and you could see for miles and miles just cornfields. I don't know how people don't get lost when everything looks exactly the same. We stopped at this little diner off the highway, and I had the best slice of cherry pie. The crust was so flaky, not soggy at all. I hate a soggy pie crust. It ruins the whole thing. I tried to make one from scratch once, but I used too much water and the dough just turned into paste. \n\nBut no, nobody is putting thoughts in my head or controlling me. It's just my own thinking.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail why you were so sure that the other home-care agency and the library people were watching your paintings and client papers? What made you certain they were trying to steal a chance that was meant for you?\n\nPatient: Well, it was just... I don't know how to explain it other than I just got it in my head. I knew it. The other home-care agency and the library people, they were watching my paintings, and they were watching my client papers, too. I just knew they were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. People kept telling me it made no sense, but I knew they were doing it. \n\nYou know, I kept those client papers in a special folder. It was a bright yellow one, plastic, so if I spilled my coffee on it, it wouldn't ruin everything. I drink a lot of coffee. Usually just black, but sometimes I like a little splash of half-and-half. The store near my house was out of half-and-half last Tuesday, so I had to buy heavy cream. It made the coffee so rich, almost like a dessert. I try not to eat too many desserts, though. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he said my cholesterol is creeping up. He wants me to eat more oatmeal. I hate oatmeal. It's like eating wet cement. Unless you put a ton of brown sugar and raisins in it, but then that defeats the whole purpose, doesn't it? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just knew they were watching my things. I could feel it, even if nobody else believed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that most people would consider impossible or not based in reality, like being given special messages from space or having a hidden identity that no one else knows about?\n\nPatient: Messages from space? No, nothing like that. I don't have any hidden identity either, I'm just me. I mean, I'm just a personal care aide. I don't know anything about space. I did see a shooting star once, though. I was sitting on my back porch—it's screened in, because the mosquitoes get so bad in the summer. They just eat me alive. My blood must be sweet or something. I bought one of those bug zappers, the ones with the blue light? It makes this loud crack every time a bug flies into it. It scared my neighbor's cat so bad the first time he heard it, he jumped right over the fence. He's an orange tabby, very friendly, but he sheds everywhere. I tried to pet him last week and my black pants were just covered in orange fur. I had to use half a roll of lint tape before I could go to work. You can't show up to a client's house looking like a mess. \n\nBut no, no messages from space or secret identities or anything impossible like that. Just the library people watching my things, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that most people would find completely impossible or out of touch with reality, even if they tried to explain things to you?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've had anything completely impossible happen. But like I told you before, I did get it in my head that the other home-care agency and some people from the library were watching my paintings and my client papers. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. Folks kept telling me it made no sense, but I still knew they were doing it. \n\nI don't know why people from the library would even care about paintings, honestly. The library downtown is so quiet, you know? I used to go there when my daughter was little to check out those picture books. The ones with the shiny covers. They always smelled like old paper and floor wax. I love the smell of floor wax. It reminds me of the elementary school I went to. Mr. Henderson was the janitor, and he always had a big ring of keys jingling on his belt. He let me help him clap the chalkboard erasers outside once. You don't see chalkboards much anymore, do you? Everything is whiteboards and those special markers. They dry out so fast if you don't put the cap back on tight. I bought a pack for my kitchen calendar to write down my shifts, but the red one dried up in two days. I was so mad. They cost like six dollars at the pharmacy. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just knew they were watching my things, even if people said it wasn't real.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I... I don't think so. Nothing completely impossible like that. I mean, I know what's real and what isn't, mostly. Except for the library people, like I said. But nothing like... I don't know, breathing underwater or flying. 

Speaking of flying, I always wanted to go on an airplane, but the tickets are just so expensive. My cousin Brenda went to Florida last winter and she said the turbulence was awful. Just bumping around up there in the clouds. I don't know if my stomach could handle it. I get carsick if I read in the passenger seat, you know? Even just looking at my phone to check a text message, my stomach starts doing flips. I have to keep some ginger candies in my purse. They're the chewy kind, with real ginger in them. They're kind of spicy, but they help. I get them at this little health food store near the bus stop. The lady who runs it always wears these big chunky wooden necklaces. They clack together when she walks. I asked her where she got them once, and she said she makes them herself out of driftwood she finds at the beach. I haven't been to the beach in years. The sand always gets everywhere, in your shoes, in your car... it's a nightmare to vacuum up. 

But no, I haven't believed anything totally impossible. Just what I told you before.

He used to get these terrible sunburns, though, peeling all over his shoulders like a snake shedding its skin. He never would put on sunscreen, said it smelled like coconuts and chemicals. I always use the unscented kind myself. I have very sensitive skin, you see. If I use anything with perfume in it, I break out in these little red bumps all along my collarbone. It's just... well, like I've been trying to explain, it's about the watching. Cerulean, navy, aqua... there are so many shades. I had this one tube of paint, ultramarine, that I bought at a little art supply store that used to be on Main Street. I was cleaning a stainless steel fridge at my client's house last week—Mrs. Gable, she's ninety-two and sharp as a tack, but she can't reach the top shelves anymore. I had to use a step stool, and one of the legs wobbled. I almost fell right into the kitchen island. It's granite, you know. Very hard. If I hit my head on that, I'd have a knot the size of a grapefruit. I love grapefruit, especially the ruby red ones with a little sprinkle of sugar on top. My grandmother used to serve them in these special little glass bowls with a serrated spoon. \n\nBut anyway, no, it wasn't impossible. It was just the truth about the library people and the agency. I knew what they were up to, no matter what anyone else said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts were not entirely your own, or that something outside of you was trying to control what you think or do?\n\nPatient: No, I've never felt anything like that. My thoughts are my own. I mean, nobody's controlling what I think. I do think a lot, though. I'm always thinking about what I need to get done at work. I have a list I keep in my purse. It's a leather purse, real heavy even when it's empty. My daughter bought it for me for Mother's Day a few years ago. She lives out in Ohio now. It's so flat out there. We drove through once when she was little, and you could see for miles and miles just cornfields. I don't know how people don't get lost when everything looks exactly the same. We stopped at this little diner off the highway, and I had the best slice of cherry pie. The crust was so flaky, not soggy at all. I hate a soggy pie crust. It ruins the whole thing. I tried to make one from scratch once, but I used too much water and the dough just turned into paste. \n\nBut no, nobody is putting thoughts in my head or controlling me. It's just my own thinking.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail why you were so sure that the other home-care agency and the library people were watching your paintings and client papers? The other home-care agency and the library people, they were watching my paintings, and they were watching my client papers, too. I just knew they were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. People kept telling me it made no sense, but I knew they were doing it. \n\nYou know, I kept those client papers in a special folder. It was a bright yellow one, plastic, so if I spilled my coffee on it, it wouldn't ruin everything. I drink a lot of coffee. Usually just black, but sometimes I like a little splash of half-and-half. The store near my house was out of half-and-half last Tuesday, so I had to buy heavy cream. It made the coffee so rich, almost like a dessert. I try not to eat too many desserts, though. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he said my cholesterol is creeping up. He wants me to eat more oatmeal. I hate oatmeal. It's like eating wet cement. Unless you put a ton of brown sugar and raisins in it, but then that defeats the whole purpose, doesn't it? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just knew they were watching my things. I could feel it, even if nobody else believed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that most people would consider impossible or not based in reality, like being given special messages from space or having a hidden identity that no one else knows about?\n\nPatient: Messages from space? No, nothing like that. I don't have any hidden identity either, I'm just me. I mean, I'm just a personal care aide. I don't know anything about space. I did see a shooting star once, though. I was sitting on my back porch—it's screened in, because the mosquitoes get so bad in the summer. They just eat me alive. My blood must be sweet or something. I bought one of those bug zappers, the ones with the blue light? It makes this loud crack every time a bug flies into it. It scared my neighbor's cat so bad the first time he heard it, he jumped right over the fence. He's an orange tabby, very friendly, but he sheds everywhere. I tried to pet him last week and my black pants were just covered in orange fur. I had to use half a roll of lint tape before I could go to work. You can't show up to a client's house looking like a mess. \n\nBut no, no messages from space or secret identities or anything impossible like that. Just the library people watching my things, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that most people would find completely impossible or out of touch with reality, even if they tried to explain things to you?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've had anything completely impossible happen. But like I told you before, I did get it in my head that the other home-care agency and some people from the library were watching my paintings and my client papers. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. Folks kept telling me it made no sense, but I still knew they were doing it. \n\nI don't know why people from the library would even care about paintings, honestly. The library downtown is so quiet, you know? I used to go there when my daughter was little to check out those picture books. The ones with the shiny covers. They always smelled like old paper and floor wax. I love the smell of floor wax. It reminds me of the elementary school I went to. Mr. Henderson was the janitor, and he always had a big ring of keys jingling on his belt. He let me help him clap the chalkboard erasers outside once. You don't see chalkboards much anymore, do you? Everything is whiteboards and those special markers. They dry out so fast if you don't put the cap back on tight. I bought a pack for my kitchen calendar to write down my shifts, but the red one dried up in two days. I was so mad. They cost like six dollars at the pharmacy. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just knew they were watching my things, even if people said it wasn't real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that even you realized was completely impossible or didn’t make any sense in the real world, something that most people would find totally unbelievable?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. Nothing completely impossible like that. I mean, I know what's real and what isn't, mostly. Except for the library people, like I said. But nothing like... I don't know, breathing underwater or flying. \n\nSpeaking of flying, I always wanted to go on an airplane, but the tickets are just so expensive. My cousin Brenda went to Florida last winter and she said the turbulence was awful. Just bumping around up there in the clouds. I don't know if my stomach could handle it. I get carsick if I read in the passenger seat, you know? Even just looking at my phone to check a text message, my stomach starts doing flips. I have to keep some ginger candies in my purse. They're the chewy kind, with real ginger in them. They're kind of spicy, but they help. I get them at this little health food store near the bus stop. The lady who runs it always wears these big chunky wooden necklaces. They clack together when she walks. I asked her where she got them once, and she said she makes them herself out of driftwood she finds at the beach. I haven't been to the beach in years. The sand always gets everywhere, in your shoes, in your car... it's a nightmare to vacuum up. \n\nBut no, I haven't believed anything totally impossible. Just what I told you before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And my client papers, too. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. \n\nI used to paint a lot of landscapes, mostly water scenes. I love the color blue. Cerulean, navy, aqua... there are so many shades. I had this one tube of paint, ultramarine, that I bought at a little art supply store that used to be on Main Street. It closed down, though, maybe five years ago? The owner, Mr. Henderson, he retired and moved to Arizona. Said his arthritis couldn't take the cold winters here anymore. I don't blame him. My knees ache terribly when it rains. I have to take a hot bath with Epsom salts just to get moving some mornings. The lavender scented salts are the best, very relaxing. I tried the eucalyptus once, but it just smelled like cough drops. Reminded me of being sick as a kid. My mother would rub Vicks VapoRub all over my chest and make me wear an old wool sock around my neck. It was terribly itchy. \n\nBut yes, even with everyone telling me I was wrong, I knew they were watching my things. I just knew it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that even you realized was very strange or didn’t make sense in the real world, something that most people would find completely impossible?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I don't think it's impossible. People said it was strange, sure. They told me it made absolutely no sense at all. But I just got it in my head that the other home-care agency and those people from the library were watching my paintings. And my client papers, too. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. And even when folks told me it made no sense, I still knew they were doing it. \n\nI actually used to keep my paintings in a big cardboard box under my bed. It was a refrigerator box, from when my neighbor got a new Frigidaire. It was avocado green. You don't see appliances in those colors anymore. Everything now is stainless steel. It shows every single fingerprint. I was cleaning a stainless steel fridge at my client's house last week—Mrs. Gable, she's ninety-two and sharp as a tack, but she can't reach the top shelves anymore. I had to use a step stool, and one of the legs wobbled. I almost fell right into the kitchen island. It's granite, you know. Very hard. If I hit my head on that, I'd have a knot the size of a grapefruit. I love grapefruit, especially the ruby red ones with a little sprinkle of sugar on top. My grandmother used to serve them in these special little glass bowls with a serrated spoon. \n\nBut anyway, no, it wasn't impossible. It was just the truth about the library people and the agency. I knew what they were up to, no matter what anyone else said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts were not entirely your own, or that something outside of you was trying to control what you think or do?\n\nPatient: No, I've never felt anything like that. My thoughts are my own. I mean, nobody's controlling what I think. I do think a lot, though. I'm always thinking about what I need to get done at work. I have a list I keep in my purse. It's a leather purse, real heavy even when it's empty. My daughter bought it for me for Mother's Day a few years ago. She lives out in Ohio now. It's so flat out there. We drove through once when she was little, and you could see for miles and miles just cornfields. I don't know how people don't get lost when everything looks exactly the same. We stopped at this little diner off the highway, and I had the best slice of cherry pie. The crust was so flaky, not soggy at all. I hate a soggy pie crust. It ruins the whole thing. I tried to make one from scratch once, but I used too much water and the dough just turned into paste. \n\nBut no, nobody is putting thoughts in my head or controlling me. It's just my own thinking.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail why you were so sure that the other home-care agency and the library people were watching your paintings and client papers? What made you certain they were trying to steal a chance that was meant for you?\n\nPatient: Well, it was just... I don't know how to explain it other than I just got it in my head. I knew it. The other home-care agency and the library people, they were watching my paintings, and they were watching my client papers, too. I just knew they were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. People kept telling me it made no sense, but I knew they were doing it. \n\nYou know, I kept those client papers in a special folder. It was a bright yellow one, plastic, so if I spilled my coffee on it, it wouldn't ruin everything. I drink a lot of coffee. Usually just black, but sometimes I like a little splash of half-and-half. The store near my house was out of half-and-half last Tuesday, so I had to buy heavy cream. It made the coffee so rich, almost like a dessert. I try not to eat too many desserts, though. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he said my cholesterol is creeping up. He wants me to eat more oatmeal. I hate oatmeal. It's like eating wet cement. Unless you put a ton of brown sugar and raisins in it, but then that defeats the whole purpose, doesn't it? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just knew they were watching my things. I could feel it, even if nobody else believed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that most people would consider impossible or not based in reality, like being given special messages from space or having a hidden identity that no one else knows about?\n\nPatient: Messages from space? No, nothing like that. I don't have any hidden identity either, I'm just me. I mean, I'm just a personal care aide. I don't know anything about space. I did see a shooting star once, though. I was sitting on my back porch—it's screened in, because the mosquitoes get so bad in the summer. They just eat me alive. My blood must be sweet or something. I bought one of those bug zappers, the ones with the blue light? It makes this loud crack every time a bug flies into it. It scared my neighbor's cat so bad the first time he heard it, he jumped right over the fence. He's an orange tabby, very friendly, but he sheds everywhere. I tried to pet him last week and my black pants were just covered in orange fur. I had to use half a roll of lint tape before I could go to work. Mr. Henderson was the janitor, and he always had a big ring of keys jingling on his belt. He let me help him clap the chalkboard erasers outside once. You don't see chalkboards much anymore, do you? Everything is whiteboards and those special markers. They dry out so fast if you don't put the cap back on tight. I bought a pack for my kitchen calendar to write down my shifts, but the red one dried up in two days. I was so mad. I don't know, breathing underwater or flying. \n\nSpeaking of flying, I always wanted to go on an airplane, but the tickets are just so expensive. My cousin Brenda went to Florida last winter and she said the turbulence was awful. Just bumping around up there in the clouds. I don't know if my stomach could handle it. I get carsick if I read in the passenger seat, you know? Even just looking at my phone to check a text message, my stomach starts doing flips. I have to keep some ginger candies in my purse. They're the chewy kind, with real ginger in them. They're kind of spicy, but they help. I get them at this little health food store near the bus stop. The lady who runs it always wears these big chunky wooden necklaces. They clack together when she walks. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like a broken record, doctor, but it's just what I keep telling you. The only thing was that whole mess with the other home-care agency and those library people. I just got it in my head that they were watching my paintings and my client papers, because they wanted to steal a chance that was meant for me. And I know folks kept telling me it didn't make a lick of sense, that it was impossible for them to be doing that, but I still knew they were. It was real to me. 

I don't even know how they'd see the paintings anyway, since I keep them in the spare room. I like doing watercolors, mostly. Well, sometimes acrylics, but the acrylics dry out so fast. You get those little tubes from the craft store, and the caps get all crusty with paint, you know? You can't even twist them off. I had to use a pair of pliers from the kitchen drawer just to open a tube of titanium white last month. The pliers are getting rusty, honestly. I think they got left out on the patio by accident. The rain we had last week was just awful, wasn't it? It washed out my neighbor's whole petunia bed. She was out there in her yellow galoshes trying to save them, but it was just mud everywhere. Mud is so hard to get out of carpets. I always make my clients take their shoes off at the door. You track that in, and you're scrubbing for hours with that foaming carpet spray. The one that smells like fake lemons. It gives me a terrible headache if I don't open a window.

My grandmother used to serve them in these special little glass bowls with a serrated spoon. \n\nBut anyway, no, it wasn't impossible. It was just the truth about the library people and the agency. I knew what they were up to, no matter what anyone else said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts were not entirely your own, or that something outside of you was trying to control what you think or do?\n\nPatient: No, I've never felt anything like that. My thoughts are my own. I mean, nobody's controlling what I think. I do think a lot, though. I'm always thinking about what I need to get done at work. I have a list I keep in my purse. It's a leather purse, real heavy even when it's empty. My daughter bought it for me for Mother's Day a few years ago. She lives out in Ohio now. It's so flat out there. We drove through once when she was little, and you could see for miles and miles just cornfields. I don't know how people don't get lost when everything looks exactly the same. We stopped at this little diner off the highway, and I had the best slice of cherry pie. The crust was so flaky, not soggy at all. I hate a soggy pie crust. It ruins the whole thing. I tried to make one from scratch once, but I used too much water and the dough just turned into paste. \n\nBut no, nobody is putting thoughts in my head or controlling me. It's just my own thinking.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail why you were so sure that the other home-care agency and the library people were watching your paintings and client papers? People kept telling me it made no sense, but I knew they were doing it. \n\nYou know, I kept those client papers in a special folder. The store near my house was out of half-and-half last Tuesday, so I had to buy heavy cream. It made the coffee so rich, almost like a dessert. I try not to eat too many desserts, though. My doctor—my regular doctor, Dr. Evans—he said my cholesterol is creeping up. He wants me to eat more oatmeal. I hate oatmeal. It's like eating wet cement. Unless you put a ton of brown sugar and raisins in it, but then that defeats the whole purpose, doesn't it? \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just knew they were watching my things. I could feel it, even if nobody else believed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that most people would consider impossible or not based in reality, like being given special messages from space or having a hidden identity that no one else knows about?\n\nPatient: Messages from space? No, nothing like that. I don't have any hidden identity either, I'm just me. I mean, I'm just a personal care aide. I don't know anything about space. I did see a shooting star once, though. I was sitting on my back porch—it's screened in, because the mosquitoes get so bad in the summer. They just eat me alive. My blood must be sweet or something. I bought one of those bug zappers, the ones with the blue light? It makes this loud crack every time a bug flies into it. It scared my neighbor's cat so bad the first time he heard it, he jumped right over the fence. He's an orange tabby, very friendly, but he sheds everywhere. I tried to pet him last week and my black pants were just covered in orange fur. I had to use half a roll of lint tape before I could go to work. You can't show up to a client's house looking like a mess. \n\nBut no, no messages from space or secret identities or anything impossible like that. Just the library people watching my things, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that most people would find completely impossible or out of touch with reality, even if they tried to explain things to you?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've had anything completely impossible happen. But like I told you before, I did get it in my head that the other home-care agency and some people from the library were watching my paintings and my client papers. They were trying to steal a chance that was meant for me. Folks kept telling me it made no sense, but I still knew they were doing it. \n\nI don't know why people from the library would even care about paintings, honestly. The library downtown is so quiet, you know? I used to go there when my daughter was little to check out those picture books. The ones with the shiny covers. They always smelled like old paper and floor wax. I love the smell of floor wax. It reminds me of the elementary school I went to. Mr. Henderson was the janitor, and he always had a big ring of keys jingling on his belt. He let me help him clap the chalkboard erasers outside once. You don't see chalkboards much anymore, do you? Everything is whiteboards and those special markers. They dry out so fast if you don't put the cap back on tight. I bought a pack for my kitchen calendar to write down my shifts, but the red one dried up in two days. I was so mad. They cost like six dollars at the pharmacy. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just knew they were watching my things, even if people said it wasn't real.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that even you realized was completely impossible or didn’t make any sense in the real world, something that most people would find totally unbelievable?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. Nothing completely impossible like that. I mean, I know what's real and what isn't, mostly. Except for the library people, like I said. But nothing like... I don't know, breathing underwater or flying. \n\nSpeaking of flying, I always wanted to go on an airplane, but the tickets are just so expensive. My cousin Brenda went to Florida last winter and she said the turbulence was awful. Just bumping around up there in the clouds. I don't know if my stomach could handle it. I get carsick if I read in the passenger seat, you know? Even just looking at my phone to check a text message, my stomach starts doing flips. I have to keep some ginger candies in my purse. They're the chewy kind, with real ginger in them. They're kind of spicy, but they help. I get them at this little health food store near the bus stop. The lady who runs it always wears these big chunky wooden necklaces. They clack together when she walks. I asked her where she got them once, and she said she makes them herself out of driftwood she finds at the beach. I haven't been to the beach in years. The sand always gets everywhere, in your shoes, in your car... it's a nightmare to vacuum up. \n\nBut no, I haven't believed anything totally impossible. Just what I told you before.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something that even you realized was completely impossible or didn’t make any sense in the real world, something that most people would find totally unbelievable?\n\nPatient: I feel like a broken record, doctor, but it's just what I keep telling you. The only thing was that whole mess with the other home-care agency and those library people. I just got it in my head that they were watching my paintings and my client papers, because they wanted to steal a chance that was meant for me. And I know folks kept telling me it didn't make a lick of sense, that it was impossible for them to be doing that, but I still knew they were. It was real to me. \n\nI don't even know how they'd see the paintings anyway, since I keep them in the spare room. I like doing watercolors, mostly. Well, sometimes acrylics, but the acrylics dry out so fast. You get those little tubes from the craft store, and the caps get all crusty with paint, you know? You can't even twist them off. I had to use a pair of pliers from the kitchen drawer just to open a tube of titanium white last month. The pliers are getting rusty, honestly. I think they got left out on the patio by accident. The rain we had last week was just awful, wasn't it? It washed out my neighbor's whole petunia bed. She was out there in her yellow galoshes trying to save them, but it was just mud everywhere. Mud is so hard to get out of carpets. I always make my clients take their shoes off at the door. You track that in, and you're scrubbing for hours with that foaming carpet spray. The one that smells like fake lemons. It gives me a terrible headache if I don't open a window.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
